UBER — Evidence appendix
All harvested proof points · company first, then peers

Evidence

Claims by section
appendix-a
  • Mobility 25% revenue take rate times a 45% Adjusted EBITDA margin gives 11.25% of Gross Bookings; Delivery 15% times 30% gives 4.50%; total company 7.00% of Gross Bookings
business-model
  • Uber operates three marketplaces that share one app, one identity layer, one payments stack and one membership, and little else.
  • On Gross Bookings, Mobility was 50.4% and Delivery 47.0% of FY2025 volume, because Delivery is presented net in more markets; Freight was 2.6%, because Gross Bookings for that segment is defined as Freight revenue and not a transaction value.
  • The measure that survives both moves is revenue less Platform Participant direct transaction costs, Uber's cut of a booking dollar after everything paid to Drivers and to end-users as incentives, because agent markets net Driver pay above the revenue line and principal markets net it below.
  • In Q2 2026 Uber returned 131bp of Gross Bookings in price and removed 167bp of non-earner cost, netting 36bp of segment margin.
  • Charging Mobility its Gross Bookings share of the $2,708M unallocated corporate line takes FY2025 from 8.1% to 6.7% of Gross Bookings and Q2 2026 from 7.6% to 5.7%, about $0.19 a trip.
  • Delivery revenue equals Gross Bookings times a marketplace take rate plus advertising revenue, and advertising is outside the Gross Bookings denominator, so rising advertising penetration lifts the reported Revenue Margin without any change in marketplace economics.
  • Against $3,572M of FY2025 segment profit, $1.7-2.0bn at full flow-through is 48% to 56%.
  • Grocery & Retail is the fastest-moving mix item: from over $1bn annualised in Q3 2020 to $7bn at Q4 2023, about $12bn at Q3 2025, and "a $15 billion category … growing over 40%" in September 2026 (Goldman Sachs Communacopia, 2026-09-10), roughly 14% of Delivery Gross Bookings annualised off Q2 2026.
  • Three years, $27bn of additional annual Gross Bookings, and the margin after participant costs moved 42bp and then stopped. The marketplace take on orders declined about 110bp over two years, on a 90-130bp band across the advertising estimate, and advertising filled the hole.
  • Capital intensity is close to nil today and the cost base is close to fully variable.
competitive-advantage
  • Uber's Mobility advantage is real and earns returns well above its cost of capital, but it is a density-and-efficiency advantage and not a pricing one, and it is defensible for now while remaining open to erosion.
  • Uber Mobility earned 8.1% of Gross Bookings in FY2025 as Segment Adjusted EBITDA. Grab's Mobility segment, on the same pre-corporate-cost basis, earned 8.7% of GMV, $690M on $7,901M, a higher margin on roughly one-twelfth the volume.
  • Uber's own February 2022 Investor Day set a long-term Mobility target of "over 10% of GBs and EBITDA margin"; four and a half years later, with Mobility Gross Bookings 2.7x the FY2021 level, the segment is at 8.1% on that measure and management no longer repeats the target.
  • Once a rival has enough drivers in a given city at a given hour to hit a competitive ETA, the incremental value of Uber's global network to that city's rider is close to zero.
  • Today AV is under 0.5% of trips, so Uber brings the scarce asset; when an AV operator reaches enough vehicles in a city to run its own liquidity, the demand Uber supplies becomes marginal and the take rate becomes negotiable.
  • Against a cost of capital in the high single digits, these returns are high and rising. The denominator is small because the business needs almost no capital, and so does an entrant.
  • Uber Mobility does not earn the best margin in its own peer set on the measure that strips out accounting, and Grab earns more on one-twelfth the bookings.
  • Uber Delivery earns an excess return on the bookings it processes and the gap over the delivery peers that disclose one has widened in each of the last four years.
  • In FY2025 the segment converted 3.93% of its $90,864M of Gross Bookings into Segment Adjusted EBITDA. DoorDash converted 2.72% of Marketplace GOV, Delivery Hero's in-scope assets 2.62% of GMV, and Grab's Deliveries segment 2.26% of GMV in Q2 2026.
  • On the like-for-like construction the headline 1.2-point advantage disappears, and on revenue the direction reverses.
  • The capital that moved through one incumbent's segment profit in a single year is of the same order as the entire profit pool of the category outside China.
  • The cross-platform combination on its own does not produce Uber's margin. What does is the advertising business that a large cross-platform consumer base makes possible at Uber's scale and that Grab is only now building
  • Uber Freight does not hold a durable competitive advantage.
  • Uber Freight does not set the spread between what a shipper pays and what a carrier is paid. The spot market does, and in an up-cycle the increase passes straight through.
  • The business needs a net revenue margin of about 10.8% to cover its own cost base and earned 10.1%.
  • It employs about $2.6bn of net assets against $5,099M of revenue, roughly 50%. C.H. Robinson employs $1,685M against $16,233M, roughly 10%, and earned a 4.9% operating margin and a 32.5% ROIC in FY2025.
competitive-landscape
  • The ride-hail market is not one market. It is a set of city- and country-level duopolies or near-monopolies
  • Uber Mobility is 48.7% of the named five and 72.3% of the remainder excluding DiDi.
  • That $200bn is one to two orders of magnitude below the "multiple multi-trillion dollar markets" management uses, and Uber's own 10-K explains the gap, because most of that spend is personal vehicle ownership.
  • Q2 2026 was the first quarter in five years in which Lyft's bookings growth exceeded Uber Mobility's, 22.6% against 22.0%, and it followed a quarter in which Uber was 5.8 points ahead.
  • Uber's last quantified category-position disclosure was February 2022: "over 80% of our current gross bookings are generated in markets where we have over a great 65% category position."
  • Uber Delivery is the second-largest disclosed delivery marketplace by gross bookings and the most profitable of the five on bookings before corporate overhead.
  • The platform layer keeps very little of the gross-bookings dollar. Adding the FY2025 profit of the five disclosed players gives $8.7bn on $299bn of bookings, 2.9%.
  • The average-order-value column spans $14.5 to $115, which is why "delivery" is three economically distinct businesses: emerging-market restaurant delivery at about $15 an order, developed-market restaurant delivery at about $30, and grocery at about $115.
  • Uber ceded 8.1 percentage points of the two-player pool between 2021 and 2025; the annual loss decelerated from 4.1pp in 2022 to 0.6pp in 2024; and almost all of the 2025 loss is Deliveroo, since excluding it Uber's share slipped 0.3pp.
  • Uber Freight is the smallest of the eleven ranked North American brokerages by gross revenue and earns the thinnest spread among the nine of those eleven that report one.
  • Every venture-funded digital brokerage Uber listed alongside itself in 2019 is gone.
financial-history
  • Gross Bookings per trip peaked at $15.10 in FY2022 and has declined for three straight years to $14.26, 5.6% below the peak, while Adjusted EBITDA per trip rose from $0.22 to $0.64 over the same three years.
  • Corporate G&A and Platform R&D was $(2,353)M in FY2023 and $(2,410)M in FY2024, up 2.4%, then $(2,708)M in FY2025, up 12.4%, and $(2,180)M in H1 2026 against $(1,842)M, up 18.3%.
forward-model
  • In the base case Adjusted EBITDA grows at 1.32x Gross Bookings growth over FY2026-FY2033, against management's February 2024 commitment to more than 2x and realised ratios of 2.86x in 2024, 1.75x in 2025 and about 1.45x in 2026.
  • The whole of the Delivery margin case is the advertising line, and the level of that line is an estimate Uber has never disclosed.
management-claims
  • The realised rate of improvement has roughly halved every two years: margin expansion on Gross Bookings was +222bp in FY2021, +166 in FY2022, +138 in FY2023, +95 in FY2024, +62 in FY2025 and +36bp annualised in H1 2026.
  • The February 2024 framework called for a mid-to-high-teens Gross Bookings CAGR, a high-30s-to-40% Adjusted EBITDA CAGR and 90%-plus annual free cash flow conversion, off an FY2023 base and running through FY2026.
management-incentives
  • on the Q4 2019 call the CFO said "long term, we remain confident about achieving our overall company adjusted EBITDA margin of 25% … we expect Rides to deliver adjusted EBITDA margin of 45% with a 25% take rate, and our Eats business to deliver adjusted EBITDA margin of 30% with a 15% take rate."
  • The July 2026 Delivery Hero deck restates it as a five-item ranking: invest in organic growth, invest in autonomous, pursue selective M&A, return excess capital, maintain investment-grade strength.
secular-trends
  • The durable driver is trip count, and trip count is consumers multiplied by frequency.
  • Within a year the composition of Uber's growth changed: about four points of trip growth were replaced by about three points of price and mix plus two points of currency.
  • The cross-platform ratio has barely moved. About one in five eligible consumers is active monthly across both businesses today, and that figure has been quoted for several years.
  • Waymo at 500,000 paid rides a week is therefore roughly **1.2% to 1.7% of Uber's US ride volume**, against the 0.5% global figure management quotes.
  • Waymo compounded tenfold in 22 months. Uber's US Mobility volume compounds in the high teens. On that arithmetic the crossover point in the top-20 US cities is not far out, and those are exactly the cities Waymo can serve first.
technology
  • The problem it solves is liquidity under variable demand: matching well across a 20x intraday swing is what lets Uber promise a short ETA without holding idle supply.
  • The scarce input is data volume and earner liquidity, not the algorithm.
  • Between FY2019 and FY2025 Uber spent $19,568M on research and development and did not displace the dependency.
  • The barrier is owning both a Mobility and a Delivery business at scale in the same market. DoorDash has DashPass and no ride-hail; Lyft has ride-hail and a far smaller delivery footprint.
  • Uber does not build an AV stack. It sells four things to firms that do: demand, fleet operations, regulatory access and financing
  • the fleet-operations layer is replicable by a smaller rival: Lyft's Flexdrive runs end-to-end fleet management for Waymo in Nashville from an 80,000 square foot depot, and there Waymo dispatches on its own app first.
value-chain
  • Consolidated, the three segments earn 5.6 cents per dollar transacted before 1.9 cents of unallocated corporate cost, and Uber's non-GAAP operating income is 3.7 cents of every dollar that moves across the platform.
  • Comparing FY2019 to FY2025 as a share of Gross Bookings, revenue rose 6.9 points and cost of revenue rose the same 6.9 points, because more markets moved to principal presentation, and the gross spread between them is identical in both years at 10.7%.
  • On the measure that nets earner payments out, Mobility's take was 20.7% of Gross Bookings in Q2 2026 against a disclosed Ridesharing Take Rate of 21% for 2018 in the IPO prospectus, on a definition that also netted Driver earnings and incentives out.
  • Uber's behaviour implies that the binding constraint in 2026 is demand elasticity and not supply cost.

Mobility 25% revenue take rate times a 45% Adjusted EBITDA margin gives 11.25% of Gross Bookings; Delivery 15% times 30% gives 4.50%; total company 7.00% of Gross Bookings

Delivery 15% 30% 4.50%. TOTAL NA 25% 7.00%. We continue to expect Mobility and Delivery to generate over 10% and roughly 5% of Gross Bookings in EBITDA margins, respectively.
— Uber Investor Day 2022, 2022-02-10

Under updated terms issued to drivers from January 2026, Uber has restructured its role so that it acts as an agent rather than the supplier of transport services outside of London. The revised contract sets out that Uber provides booking, payment processing and platform services, while drivers supply transport services directly to passengers. ... The approach cannot be applied in London. Transport for London licensing rules require private hire operators to contract directly with passengers, preventing the use of an agency structure. As a result, Uber remains the principal supplier in the capital.
— TaxiPoint, PRINCIPAL TO AGENCY: Uber restructures driver contracts outside of London to sidestep VAT hit from Rachel Reeves minicab tax reforms, 2026-01-02

filingsupports
In most cases, the Company is acting as an agent in facilitating the ability of a driver to provide a transportation service to a rider. In these cases, the Company reports revenue on a net basis, reflecting the fee owed to the Company from a driver as revenue, and not the gross amount collected from the rider. In certain markets, the Company acts as a principal for transportation services as the Company controls the services provided. Operating segments are defined as components of an entity for which separate financial information is available and that is regularly reviewed by the Chief Operating Decision Maker (“CODM”) in deciding how to allocate resources to an individual segment and in assessing performance. The Company’s Chief Executive Officer is the Company’s CODM. The CODM reviews financial information presented on a consolidated basis for purposes of making operating decisions, allocating resources, and evaluating financial performance. As such, the Company has determined that it operates in one reportable segment.

websupports
In a memo to employees, founder and CEO Dan Lewis informed them: "Today is your last day at the company." "Following an exhaustive process, spanning many, many months during which we explored all viable strategic options for the business, the result is where we are today," Lewis wrote. "Convoy is closing the doors on its current core business operations and exploring and evaluating strategic options for what might come next." The company, once valued at $3.8 billion by investors, had already downsized its workforce from 1,500 to around 500 employees and was facing imminent financial difficulties, with funds expected to run out within weeks.

On 1 November 2023, supply chain company Flexport acquired Convoy's assets for $16M and retained a small group of about fifty employees. ... In July 2025, DAT Solutions purchased the Convoy Platform from Flexport for $250M.

We define Trips as the number of completed consumer Mobility rides and Delivery orders in a given period. For example, an UberX Share ride with three paying consumers represents three unique Trips, whereas an UberX ride with three passengers represents one Trip. ... We define Gross Bookings as the total dollar value, including any applicable taxes, tolls, and fees, of: Mobility rides, Delivery orders (in each case without any adjustment for consumer discounts and refunds, Driver and Merchant earnings, and Driver incentives) and Freight revenue. Gross Bookings do not include tips earned by Drivers.
